<header>The Epistle.</header>

<header>The Epistle.</header>

*Oh, that I might first effectively recommend to you the full recovery of that precious legacy of our blessed Savior: peace. Peace with God and peace with men; next to grace, it is the best of all blessings. Yet, woe is me, it has been banished for far too long from the Christian world with such hostility, as if peace itself were the worst of enemies and deserved <note>original: "meet"</note> to be sentenced to perpetual extermination.*

*Oh, for a fountain of tears to mourn the slaughter <note>original: "slain"</note> of God’s people in every corner <note>original: "coasts"</note> of the earth! How has Christendom become a universal Field of Blood <note>original: "Aceldama"; an Aramaic term referring to the "Field of Blood" mentioned in the New Testament</note>? # The Epistle.

*...men, he who fears God and works righteousness is accepted by Him. <margin>Acts 10:35</margin> My dear brothers, let us beware <note>original: "Beware we"</note> lest, while we follow the pursuit <note>original: "chase"</note> of zeal, we out-run charity—without which piety itself would be unwelcome. As for matters of opinion regarding differences in religion, which woefully distract the whole known world (not only Christians, but all men) to the great detriment <note>original: "prejudice"</note> of millions of souls, let this be our sure rule:*

<margin>Jude 3 <note>"...contend for the faith which was once delivered unto the saints."</note></margin>

*Whoever he may be who holds the faith that was once delivered to the saints, and therefore agrees with us in all fundamental truths, let him be received as a brother. For there is but one Lord, one faith, one baptism:*

<margin>Ephesians 4:5</margin>

# The Epistle.

...to reclaim him: 

*If, after all powerful efforts <note>original: "indeavours"</note>, he insists on remaining obstinate in his evil way, let us renounce <note>original: "disclaim"</note> our fellowship with him and not consider him worthy of a God-speed <term>God-speed: a traditional blessing or greeting, implying approval or support for one's journey or actions</term>. But if he should not only willfully undermine the foundation <note>original: "ground-work"</note> of the Christian faith with his own damnable opinions, but also spread <note>original: "diffuse"</note> his heretical blasphemies to the infection of others, let him be cut off by spiritual censures <term>spiritual censures: formal punishments or rebukes issued by the church, such as excommunication</term>. Furthermore, let him be dealt with by public authority <note>civil or state legal power</note> in such a way that the harm of his contagion <note>original: "mischief of his contagion"; the author compares the spread of false teachings to a spreading disease</note> may be prevented in a timely manner, and he himself may be made aware of his heinous crime.*

In all of these proceedings, a fair distinction must be made between <note>original: "betwixt"</note> the soul that has been seduced,

<summary>The author argues for escalating discipline against those who persist in heresy, suggesting that if gentle correction fails, the offender should be excommunicated and handled by civil authorities to prevent the spread of their ideas. He notes that a distinction must be made between those who are merely misled and those who are willfully malicious.</summary>

# The Epistle.

*and the pestilent <note>original: "pestilent"; meaning harmful or morally diseased</note> seducer: the one calls for compassion, the other for severity. In the fourth place, I beseech <note>original: "beseech"; to ask or beg earnestly</note> you, let us take heed <note>pay careful attention</note> of being swayed by self-interest in all our designs. These have always been the ruin <note>original: "bane"</note> of the best undertakings, being no more plausibly insinuating <note>meaning they creep in under a seemingly reasonable or attractive guise</note> than they are pernicious <note>highly harmful or destructive</note>. For that partial self-love which naturally lodges in every man’s breast is ready to lead us toward those

# The Epistle.

*...as single persons; that the main purpose <note>original: "end"</note> of our being (next to the glory of our Creator <note>original: "maker"</note>) is a universal usefulness <note>original: "serviceablenesse"</note> to others. In achieving this, we shall far more excellently <note>original: "eminently"</note> advance our own happiness than by the best of our private, self-seeking efforts <note>original: "indeavors"</note>.*

At the same time <note>original: "But withall"</note>, it will be appropriate <note>original: "meet"</note> for us to consider that, while we are made to serve all, we must do so only in our own station <term>station: one's social position, rank, or assigned role in life</term>. There can be no order without a proper <note>original: "due"</note> hierarchy of ranks <note>original: "subordination of degrees"</note> and a diversity of callings <note>original: "vocations"</note>. <meta>This page continues the argument from the previous page regarding the necessity of social order and staying within one's designated role, using the biblical metaphor of the body to illustrate how different professions must function together without overstepping their bounds.</meta>

<margin>1 Corinthians 7:20</margin>
...and the holy instruction <note>original: "charge"</note> of the Apostle: *Let every man remain in the same calling in which he was called.* We are all members of the same body, each of which has its own specific function <note>original: "proper employment"</note>. The head is meant to direct and govern, the feet to walk, the eyes to see, and the ears to hear. How insane would we consider a man who attempted <note>original: "should affect"</note> to walk on his head, to hear with his eye, or to see with his ear? 

It is surely no less absurd <note>original: "incongruous"</note> for those in religious and civil government <note>original: "divine and civil administrations"</note> to attempt to take on and manage each other's roles <note>original: "function"</note>. In their nature and quality, such attempts are no less reckless <note>original: "desperate"</note>. Therefore, let us strive to promote the common good in such a way that a religious passion <note>original: "pious Zeal"</note> does not lead to chaos <note>original: "confusion"</note>.

<summary>The author uses the metaphor of the human body to argue that society requires a strict division of labor between religious and civil authorities. He warns that when individuals overstep their specific "calling" or professional role, it results in social absurdity and dangerous chaos.</summary>

->The Epistle.<-

*confusion*; and that we may not mistakenly build the walls of Babel while we intend to build Jerusalem: Not only religion, but also statecraft <note>original: "policie"</note> calls us to encourage all useful professions; and we should encourage the sacred professions even more, as the soul is more precious than the entire world besides. Care must therefore be taken to avoid anything that might discourage the pursuit of learning and knowledge; for without these, the world would soon be overrun with ignorance and barbarism. All arts and sciences <note>original: "All arts"</note>, therefore, being excellent in their own right, may justly claim their own privileges; and if they lack the respect that is due to them, they will quickly wither away <note>original: "languish"</note>. ->The Epistle.<-

Above all, as *Divinity* <note>Theology or the study of God.</note> is the *Queen of Sciences*, so should it be our just shame that while her handmaids <note>The secular arts and sciences that were traditionally seen as supporting the study of theology.</note> are mounted on horseback, she should wait on them on foot.

Fifthly, as it is our greatest honor that the name of *Christ* is called upon us, so let it—I beg of you—be our care that our profession of faith is not formal, empty, and barren like the *Jewish* fig tree <note>A reference to the biblical story in Mark 11, where Jesus curses a fig tree that has leaves but no fruit, symbolizing hypocrisy or lack of spiritual productivity.</note>, abounding with leaves but void of fruit. Instead, let it be real, active, fruitful in all good works, and exemplary in a universal obedience to the whole will of God. For it is a scandal that can never be lamented enough, that any of those who are Saints by calling (as we all are, or should be <note>In this context, "Saints" refers to the general body of Christian believers called to holiness, rather than specifically canonized figures.</note>) should hug some darling <note>original: "dearling"</note> sin

<summary>The author argues that theology should be held in higher regard than secular sciences and urges Christians to lead lives of active, fruitful obedience rather than empty religious profession. He warns that it is a great scandal for those called to be "Saints" to continue secretly cherishing their favorite sins.</summary>
<keywords>Queen of Sciences, Divinity, Christian profession, Jewish fig tree, fruitfulness, hypocrisy, Saints by calling, darling sin</keywords>

# The Epistle.

But for us, who have learned to know the great *Mystery of Godliness* <note>original: "Mysterie of Godlinesse"; a reference to 1 Timothy 3:16, meaning the essential truths of the Christian faith</note> and have committed ourselves to a strict covenant of obedience—if we should allow ourselves to be led astray into any enormous wickedness, we shall cause heaven to blush and hell to triumph. # The Epistle.

...fully in matters of religion, to keep within the proper limits of God's revealed will. This is a duty that I wish to God were not so necessary in these last days; for who does not see what spirits of error have gone out into the world to seduce simple and unstable <note>original: "ungrounded"</note> souls? Woe is me—what crowds are carried to hell by these devilish deceptions <note>original: "impostures"</note>! 

One person claims visions and revelations of new truths <note>original: "verities"</note> which the world was not worthy to know until now. Another boasts of "new lights" <note>original: "newlights"; a term often used in the 17th century for supposedly divine inspirations or radical interpretations claimed by non-conformist groups</note> of strange <note>original: "uncouth"</note> interpretations, hidden from all previous eyes. One person despises the "dead letter" <note>original: "dead letter"; the literal text of the Bible, often contrasted by radicals with the "living spirit"</note> of the Scriptures; another twists it to fit his own mistaken meaning. 

Oh, the monstrosities <note>original: "prodiges"</note> of damnable, heretical, and godless <note>original: "Atheous"</note> fantasies that have consequently plagued the Christian Church! (For which, what good soul does not mourn in secret?) You shall successfully <note>original: "happily"</note> avoid these dangers if you stay close to the written word of our God, which alone is able to make you wise enough for salvation <note>original: "wise to salvation"; a reference to 2 Timothy 3:15</note>. As our Savior...

<summary>The author warns Christians against the "spirits of error" and radical religious "new lights" of the age, urging them to stick to the literal written word of God to avoid damnation. He laments how many souls are being led astray by claims of new revelations and distorted biblical interpretations.</summary>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page concludes the author's introductory letter, urging the reader to reject radical religious innovations in favor of established scripture before offering a final blessing and closing the epistle.</meta>

...repelled the **Devil**, so you should repel the fanatical spirits of these mentally unstable <note>original: "brain-sick"</note> men with the phrase, "**It is written**." Let those who would try to be wiser than God justly perish in their own arrogance <note>original: "presumption"</note>. I stake my soul on yours if you keep yourself within **St. Paul’s** safeguard: not to be wise beyond what is written <note>A reference to 1 Corinthians 4:6, which warns against prideful speculation beyond the text of the Bible</note>. 

I could easily, out of the abundance <note>original: "exuberance"</note> of my Christian love, overwhelm <note>original: "overcharge"</note> you with a multitude of holy counsels, but I do not wish to make this farewell tedious. May the God of heaven bless these and all other wholesome warnings <note>original: "admonitions"</note> for the advancement <note>original: "furtherance"</note> of your souls in grace. And may His good Spirit ever lead and guide us in all such ways as may be pleasing to Him, until we joyfully <note>original: "happily"</note> meet to share in that incomprehensible glory which He has prepared for all His saints. Until then, farewell from your fellow pilgrim in this world of sorrow <note>original: "vale of tears"</note>,

->Joseph Hall.<-

| Higham, near | Nov. 3, |
| Norwich, | 1651. |

# THE GREAT MYSTERY OF GODLINESS.

### Section 1.

<margin>
1 Timothy 3:16.
*Great is the mystery of godliness.*
</margin>

Let no one set out to contemplate the Great Mystery of Godliness without a heart filled with awe—a heart composed of a gracious blend of love, joy, and wonder. Such a heart, O Savior, I desire to bring with me through your grace, as I meditate on that infinitely glorious work of our redemption. It was just as possible for your "chosen vessel" <note>original: "chosen Vessell"; a reference to the Apostle Paul, described in Acts 9:15 and 2 Corinthians 12:2-4 as being caught up to heaven.</note>, who was caught up into Paradise by a divine ecstasy,

<summary>The author introduces a meditation on the "Great Mystery of Godliness," arguing that the work of Christian redemption should only be approached with a heart full of love and wonder.</summary>
<keywords>Godliness, Mystery, Redemption, Savior, Paradise, Apostle Paul, 1 Timothy 3:16</keywords>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's discussion of the Apostle Paul's experience in Paradise. It contrasts the "Mystery of Godliness" with the lesser mysteries of human skill and the natural world.</meta>

Paradise, and there heard unutterable words. He was as unable to express what he saw and heard above as he was to describe what was performed by you here below. Therefore, being unable to either comprehend or speak of things that are so far beyond wonder, he contents himself with a passionate <note>original: "patheticall"; in this context, it means full of deep emotion or passion rather than its modern sense of being pitiable.</note> suggestion of that which he saw could never be admired enough: **Great is the Mystery of Godliness.**

There are great mysteries of art, which the wit and experience of skillful men have discovered. There are greater mysteries of nature, some parts of which have been described by human skill and industry, but the greater part lies hidden from mortal eyes. But these are less than nothing compared to the Great Mystery of Godliness. For what are these other mysteries but the deep secrets of the creation? <note>original: "the Creature"; meaning the created world or anything that is not God the Creator.</note> They are, therefore, insignificant and finite, just like creation itself. But the Mystery of Godliness consists of the unfathomable depths of an infinite Deity.

<summary>The author argues that the "Mystery of Godliness" is far superior to the mysteries of human art or the natural world, as it concerns the infinite nature of God rather than finite, created things.</summary>
<keywords>Apostle Paul, Paradise, Mystery of Godliness, Art, Nature, Creation, Deity, 1 Timothy 3:16</keywords>

What proportion could there be, O blessed Jesus, between a God and a man; between the finite and the infinite? Only the power of your everlasting and unmeasurable love has so brought these two together <note>original: "reduced one of these to the other"</note> that both are united in your glorious person to create an absolute Savior for mankind. --- Page 38 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from page 9, concluding the word "mystery." It explores the mechanics of the Incarnation—how Christ can be both fully God and fully man without the two natures being confused or lost.</meta>

<page-num>10</page-num>
<header>### The Great Mystery</header>

...mystery, that the infinite Deity and finite flesh should meet in one subject <note>original: "subject" — in this context, a single person or individual being.</note>? Yet this happened in such a way that the humanity should not be absorbed by the Godhead, nor the Godhead restricted <note>original: "coarcted" — constrained, cramped, or pressed together.</note> by the humanity, but both are inseparably united. The Godhead is not turned into humanity, and the humanity is not turned into God, yet both are indivisibly joined. They are joined in such a way that there is no confusion of the two, and distinguished in such a way that there is no division between them. 

In this way, O God, you were manifested in the flesh, so that you—the Word of your eternal Father—were made flesh and <margin>John 1:14</margin> lived among us. We humans beheld your glory, the glory as of the only begotten of the Father, full of grace and truth. Yet you were made flesh not by being converted into flesh, but by the taking on <note>original: "assumption" — the theological term for the divine nature taking human nature into itself.</note> of flesh into your eternal Deity. This was a taking on, not into the nature of the Godhead, but into the person of you, who are God.

<summary>The author explains the doctrine of the Incarnation, emphasizing that Christ's divine and human natures coexist perfectly without one destroying the other. This union occurs within the single person of God the Son, rather than by changing the essence of His divinity.</summary>

<keywords>Incarnation, Christology, Deity, humanity, John 1:14, hypostatic union, Godhead, assumption of flesh</keywords>

### — SECTION III.

But, O Savior, was it not enough for you to be manifested in flesh? Did not that physical <note>original: "elementary" — referring to the four elements that were believed to compose the human body.</note> composition carry in it enough humiliation <note>original: "abasement"</note> without any further addition? (Since for God to become man was a greater act than for all of creation to be reduced <note>original: "redacted"</note> to nothing.) I can hear <margin>Job 25:6</margin> *Bildad* the *Shuhite* say, "Man is a worm"; and I hear him, who was a noble foreshadowing <note>original: "Type" — a biblical person or event believed to foreshadow Christ; here likely referring to King David.</note> of you, say,

# The Great Mystery

...the great mystery of godliness: that you, who are the God of spirits, were seen by those heavenly spirits while clothed in flesh. It was certainly great news to the angels to see their God born and interacting as a man among men. For a person to see an angel is a matter of great wonder; but for an angel to see God become a man is an even greater wonder. This is because in this instance, the change involves an infinite subject, whereas in the other, it involves a finite, though incorporeal <term>incorporeal: having no physical body or substance</term> being.

But pause here for a moment, O my soul, and inquire a little into these strange spectators: "Seen by angels"? Who or what might those be? Are there any such real, incorporeal, permanent substances <note>original: "substances"; here meaning distinct, existing entities</note>, or are they only things of the imagination and temporary representations of the Almighty’s will? Woe is me—so that no error may be missing from this monstrous <note>original: "prodigious"</note> age—do we live to see a revival <note>original: "reviviction"</note>...

# Of Godliness

## Of Godliness

...that the angels are truly existing, spiritual, intelligent, powerful, everlasting <term>eviternal: a philosophical and theological term describing beings that have a beginning in time but will never have an end</term> creatures, whose being is not exposed to our senses, but evidenced both to our faith and reason; not confined <note>original: "circumscribed"</note> to any coarse <note>original: "grosse"</note> physical location, but truly existing where they are, and acting according to their spiritual nature.

Of these angels, O blessed Savior, you were seen manifested in the flesh, to their wonder and joy <note>original: "gratulation"</note>. # The PREFACE.

Just as those who move from their old home and set out to live in another country where they are certain to settle usually forget the faces and customs they were formerly used to, and instead apply themselves to getting to know those with whom they will afterwards associate; so it is here with me. 

Since I am about to leave my earthly <term>Tabernacle</term><note>original: "Tabernacle"; a biblical reference (2 Corinthians 5:1) comparing the human body to a temporary tent or dwelling place.</note>, in which I have spent the few and difficult days of my <term>pilgrimage</term><note>A common historical metaphor for the journey of a Christian's life toward heaven.</note>, and move to an enduring city above, I have desired to become acquainted with that *invisible* world to which I am going. I wish to get to know my good God and His blessed angels and saints, with whom I hope to spend a happy eternity. And if, through frequent and serious meditation, I have attained (by God's mercy) some degree of enlightened understand-

<sig>B 2</sig>

<summary>In this opening to the preface, the author compares his approaching death to moving to a new country, expressing his desire to study the "invisible world" of heaven before he arrives.</summary>

<keywords>afterlife, meditation, pilgrimage, tabernacle, heaven, spirituality</keywords>

<header>The Preface.</header>

-standing of them and their blissful condition, I thought it would be nothing other than profitable to share this with my fellow pilgrims. *And*, knowing that we can never be <term>sensible</term><note>original: "sensible"; meaning to be fully aware or to feel something deeply.</note> enough of our own *happiness* unless we also *know* our own dangers and the woeful failures of others; nor can we so fully bless our eyes with the *sight of heaven* if we do not also cast some glances toward *hell*; I have considered it necessary to spend some time thinking about that dreadful region of darkness and confusion. By focusing on the former—the joys of heaven—our desires may be sharpened to enjoy that blessedness. By the other—the terrors of hell—we may be motivated to take care to avoid those paths that lead down to that <term>second death</term><note>A theological term referring to eternal damnation or final separation from God, found in the biblical Book of Revelation.</note>, and to feel a continual thankfulness toward that merciful God, whose infinite goodness has delivered us from that pit of horror and <term>perdition</term><note>original: "perdition"; a state of eternal punishment and damnation.</note>.

<summary>The author explains that he will discuss both heaven and hell to sharpen the reader’s desire for salvation and their gratitude toward God for escaping damnation.</summary>

# THE INVISIBLE WORLD.

#### SECTION I.

*That there is an invisible world.*

How can anyone think otherwise than that the great God of heaven loses much glory through our ignorance? For how can we give Him the honor due to His name while we conceive of Him and His works too narrowly? To know Him as He is, is beyond the capacity of our <term>finite</term><note>original: "finite"; meaning limited or having bounds, as opposed to God's infinite nature.</note> understanding; we...

Of all others, the Sadducees would have been the most dull and foolish <note>original: "sottish"</note> heretics that ever existed if—as some have interpreted their views—they had completely denied the existence of any spirits. Certainly, as the learned Cameron argues on their behalf, they could not have been so senseless. Since they believed in the books of Moses and were aware of their own life and movement <note>original: "animation"</note>, their own hearts <note>original: "bosoms"</note> must have convinced them of their own spiritual inhabitant <note>original: "inmate," referring to the soul within the body</note>. After all, what but a spirit could enable them to argue against spirits? And how could they believe in a God and yet deny the existence of spirit? 

It was bad enough that they denied the immortality and permanent existence <note>original: "constant subsistence"</note> of those angelic, immaterial beings <note>original: "substances"</note>. This is an opinion that was long ago rejected with scorn <note>original: "hissed out"</note>, not only by the school of Christianity but even by the lowest ranks <note>original: "stalls and sties"</note> of the most crude <note>original: "brutish"</note> paganism. However, not very long ago (as reported by Hosius <note>Stanislaus Hosius (1504–1579), a Polish Cardinal and theologian.</note> and Prateolus <note>Gabriel de Préau (1511–1588), a French theologian who wrote a famous encyclopedia of heresies.</note>)...

<summary>The author argues that the Sadducees were not so foolish as to deny the existence of all spirits, as their own capacity for reason and belief in Moses proved the existence of the soul. He condemns their denial of the immortality of angels as an idea rejected even by pagans.</summary>

### Section II.
### *The Organization <note>original: "distribution"</note> of the Invisible World.*

I cannot entirely dislike the idea <note>original: "conceit"</note> of Johann Reuchlin <note>A German humanist and scholar (1455–1522) who studied the Jewish mystical tradition known as the Kabbalah.</note> and his *Kabbalah*, which was supported by Galatinus <note>Pietro Galatino (1460–1540), an Italian theologian and scholar of Hebrew.</note>. They suggest that just as in an egg, the yolk lies in the middle, surrounded by the white, and that again by a membrane and a shell; so the sensory world <term>sensory world</term><gloss>The "sensible" world; the physical realm we can perceive with our five senses.</gloss> is enclosed within the intellectual world <term>intellectual world</term><gloss>The "intelligible" world; the spiritual realm understood through the mind and soul rather than the senses.</gloss>. 

However, I must add that this is not merely a matter of one being wrapped inside the other <note>original: "involution"</note>. Rather, it is a spiritual soaking-through <note>original: "permeation"</note> and an internal existence <note>original: "inexistence," here meaning existing within something else rather than "not existing."</note>. Yet, this happens without any mixing of their substances and without any confusion. For those pure and simple

Pure <note>original: "ſimple"; in this context, meaning uncompounded or not made of mixed elements.</note> natures are not capable of mingling with coarse <note>original: "groſſe"</note>, material substances. The God of Order has given them their own separate essences, duties <note>original: "offices"</note>, and activities <note>original: "operations" </note>, both for the management of their own spiritual commonwealth within themselves and for the arranging, governing, and moving of this physical world <term>sensible world</term><gloss>The "sensible" world refers to the physical realm that can be perceived by the five senses.</gloss>. 

Just as we would be badly mistaken about a man if we thought he was nothing but a body because our eyes see nothing more, so we would err just as badly if, while beholding this outward structure <note>original: "fabrick"</note>, we should imagine that there is nothing in this vast <term>Universe</term> but the mere lifeless substance of the heavens and the elements which meet our sight. Those lively and active powers that dwell within them could not be what they are if they were not purely spiritual.

<margin>Book 1.</margin> We encounter those immaterial and invisible powers, who receive their origin and continued existence, their natures, and their duties <note>original: "offices"</note> from that King of Glory. Each one of these beings is so mighty that they represent a world of power on their own; each one is so knowledgeable that they contain a world of wisdom. They are so countlessly many that their number is almost infinite. Yet, this countless multitude is so perfectly united in one heavenly community <term>celestial polity</term><gloss>A "polity" or "politie" refers to an organized society or system of government; here, it describes the hierarchical and harmonious order of the angels.</gloss> that their entire fellowship—under the laws and government of their sovereign Creator—makes them a complete world of spirits, invisibly living and moving both within and above this visible globe of the material world. 

After these, we meet with the glorified souls of the righteous <note>original: "the Just"</note>, who have now been released from this earthly body <note>original: "prison of clay"; a common metaphor for the physical human body as a temporary, heavy vessel for the soul.</note> and enjoy the full liberty of heaven. At the end of time, they shall be reunited to their then-immortal bodies.

### SECTION 5
### _The Knowledge of Angels_

If Samson could have had his full strength while working in the mill when he lacked <note>original: "wanted"</note> his eyes, it would have been of little use to him; such is power without knowledge. But where both of these come together <note>original: "concur"</note> as one, how can they fail to be effective?

Which of these qualities is more prominent in the blessed spirits is not easy to determine. They are so perfectly knowledgeable that even the pagan <note>original: "heathen"</note> philosophers have called them by the name of <term>Intelligences</term> <note>In medieval philosophy, "Intelligences" were celestial beings—often identified as angels—that were thought to move the planets and possess pure, non-physical intellect.</note>, as if their very being were made up of understanding. Indeed, what is there in this whole compass of the vast universe that is hidden from their eyes? Only the private chamber <note>original: "closet"</note> of a man's heart is locked away from them, as it is reserved solely for their Maker. Yet even so, they can, through some imperceptible <note>original: "insensible"</note> cracks <note>original: "chinks"</note>

Even though they felt only the effect, we know it was an Angel that struck <note>original: "stroke"</note> them with blindness. Nothing was visible when the *Egyptian* first-born were struck dead in a single night; the astrologers of the time might say they were struck by the influence of the planets <note>original: "Planet-struck"; the belief that celestial bodies could cause sudden death or madness</note>, but we know it was done by the hand of an Angel. ### Section 8.

#### *The Appearances of Angels.*

If these heavenly <note>original: "celestiall"</note> spirits—however numerous, powerful, knowledgeable, or excellently glorious they may be—were complete strangers to us, what would their number, power, knowledge, or glory matter to us? I hear of the great riches, status, and magnificence of some remote Eastern kings <note>original: "Monarchs"</note>, but how am I any better for it, when at such a distance their behavior <note>original: "port," meaning their style or carriage</note> and affairs have no connection to me? To me, it is all the same for something not to exist at all as for it to have no concern for me. Let us therefore diligently inquire what mutual fellowship <note>original: "communion"</note> there is, or may be, between these blessed spirits and us.

And first, nothing is more plain than that the angels of God have not always been kept

<margin>Book 1.</margin> Franciscus Albertinus <note>a 17th-century Jesuit author</note> reports, citing Baronius <note>Cesare Baronius (1538–1607) was a famous Catholic cardinal and historian</note>, an event said to have happened here at home: that in the year 1601 in England, an angel was seen upon one of our altars. The angel appeared in a visible form with a bare sword in his hand, which he brandished glitteringly, sometimes thrusting <note>original: "foyning"</note> and sometimes striking, thereby threatening this kingdom with immediate destruction so long ago. 

Indeed, why should we give more credit to these claimants of visions than to Adelbertus, the German arch-heretic <note>original: "Herestarch," meaning a leader of a heretical sect</note>? He was condemned in a Council of Rome by Pope Zachary, yet he claimed just as confidently that his guardian angel appeared to him daily and provided him with many divine revelations and instructions. If there is a supposed difference between these accounts, where or how shall we find it?

## Of God and His Angels.

We know this: just as surely as we see men, we are certain that holy men have seen angels. <margin>Book 1.</margin>—enlightening the understanding, repelling temptations, furthering our opportunities for good, preventing occasions for sin, comforting our sorrows, quickening <note>In this context, "quickening" means reviving or giving life to.</note> our dullness, and encouraging our weakness. Lastly, after all their careful attendance here below, they convey the souls of those in their charge to their glory, presenting them into the hands of their faithful Creator.

It is somewhat too hard to believe that there have been eyewitnesses <note>original: "ocular witnesses"</note> of these happy convoys. Whoever wishes <note>original: "Who lists"</note> may believe what **Jerome** <note>Saint Jerome (c. 347–420), a Church Father best known for translating the Bible into Latin.</note> tells us: that **Anthony the Hermit** saw the soul of **Paul**, his partner in that solitude, carried up by them to heaven; that **Severinus**, Bishop of **Cologne** <note>original: "Colein"</note>, saw the soul of **St. Martin** transported in this way, as **Gregory** reports in his *Dialogues*; or that **Benedict** saw the soul of **Germanus** in the form of a fiery globe, thus conveyed

### Section IX.

#### *The respect which we owe to the Angels.*

Such is the respect that good angels show to us; now, what is our duty to them? It was well said by one author that the life of **angels** is social <note>original: "politicall"; meaning they live in a structured community or society</note>, full of interaction among themselves and with us. What they communicate to each other during their **divine manifestations** <note>original: "Theophanies"; appearances of God or divine beings to humanity</note> is not for us to determine; but since their kind services toward us are so constant, it is fitting that we inquire what duties are required of us toward them.

<margin>Bernard, in his commentary on the Psalm *"He who dwells"* <note>original: *Qui habitat*; this refers to Psalm 91, a text often cited regarding angelic protection.</note>.</margin>

# THE INVISIBLE WORLD.

### The Second BOOK.

#### SECTION I.

#### *Of the Souls of Men.*

#### *Of their separation and Immortality.*

Following these angelic beings <note>original: "Angelicall Essences"</note>, the souls of men—whether residing in the body or separated <note>original: "severed"</note> from it—are those spirits that inhabit the invisible world. I say they are "next" to the angels; they are not the same as them, nor are they superior <note>The text ends here, continuing on the next page with the word "Though".</note>

<page-num>79</page-num>
<header>Of the Souls of Men.</header>

<margin>Section 1.</margin>
Though some of the ancient writers thought that the fall <note>original: "ruine"</note> of the angels was to be replaced <note>original: "supplyed"</note> by blessed human souls, they certainly spoke without authority <note>original: "without the book"</note>. For He who is Truth itself has said they are <term>like the angels</term> <note>original Greek: ἰσάγγελοι (isangeloi)</note>, not the same. And those thinkers are rightly rejected <note>original: "exploded"</note>—whether they were Pythagoreans, Stoics, Gnostics, Manichees, or Almaricus (or even *Lactantius* himself, if he held that error as *Ludovicus Vives* interprets <note>original: "construes"</note> him)—who falsely dreamed that the souls of men were made of the very substance of that God who inspired them. 

These errors are more suited for hellebore <note>An ancient herbal remedy used to treat madness or "purge the brain."</note> than for theological debate. Without a doubt, souls are spiritual substances, and their origin is as far removed from the physical body as heaven is from earth. *Galen* was as poor a theologian as he was a good physician when he determined the soul to be nothing more than the physical composition and tempera-
->ment<-

<summary>This page argues that human souls are distinct spiritual substances, neither filling the ranks of fallen angels nor being composed of God's own divine essence. The author also refutes the materialist view of the physician Galen, who argued the soul was merely a product of the body's physical temperament.</summary>

Regarding these functions, even if the soul receives its initial stimuli from our senses and mental images <note>original: "phantasms"; in the philosophy of the time, these were the mental "pictures" created by the brain from sensory input</note> while it is within us, it still perfects and completes these operations through the inward powers of its own faculties. 

The soul can perform all these actions much more accurately when it is purely itself. This is because the burden <note>original: "clog"</note> that the body brings with it cannot help but weigh down <note>original: "pregravate"</note> and trouble the soul in all her performances. In the meantime, these acts are rightly considered purely mental actions; they do not even receive a name from the body. We walk, move, speak, see, feel, and perform other human acts; the power that does them comes from the soul, but the means or instrument by which they are done is the body. No one would say the soul itself walks or sees, but rather that the body does so by means of the soul. However, we can no more say that the soul understands or thinks by the aid of the body,

<page-num>86</page-num>
<header>Of the Souls of Men.</header>

<margin>Section 1</margin>

Now, we cannot imagine one kind of life for the head and another for the body. His state, therefore, is our own; in every way, we are conformed to him. Just as our bodies will one day be glorious like his, so too our souls—once separated <note>original: "severed"</note> by death—cannot be anything but crowned with immortality, just as his was. 

Furthermore, if he shall come to judge both the living <note>original: "quick"</note> and the dead, those dead people whom he judges must actually be alive. For as our Savior said in a similar case, God is not the judge of the dead as if they were non-existent, but the judge of the living who had died—meaning they are living through death and after death. 

And what does the Catholic Church <note>meaning the "universal" or whole Christian church, rather than a specific denomination</note> consist of, if not some members battling on earth <note>original: "warfaring"; referring to the "Church Militant"</note> and others triumphant in heaven? And what does that triumph imply, if not that they truly exist, and exist in glory? What "Communion of Saints" could there be if the souls of the departed did not exist?

<summary>The author argues that because Christ (the "head") is immortal, the souls of his followers (the "body") must also be immortal. He further notes that the "Communion of Saints" and the final judgment require that those who have died remain living in spirit.</summary>

<page-num>87</page-num>
<header>Of the Souls of Men.</header>

<margin>Book 2.</margin>
...and would it make sense for the soul to cease to exist just when it begins to reach perfection? What would be the purpose of the resurrection of the body, if not to reunite with its old partner, the soul? Such a reunion necessarily implies that there was both a separation and a continued existence for both parts.

Finally, what kind of life can truly exist except for the life of the soul? And how can that life be everlasting if it is not continuous? Or how can it be continuous if it does not actually exist during the interval of death? While someone might still be considered a human being, he certainly cannot be considered a Christian if he is more certain that he has a soul within his body now than he is that his soul will one day exist outside of his body. Death may act like a tyrant over our earthly parts, but the worst it can do to our spiritual part is to set it free from a "friendly bondage" <note>the author views the physical body as a temporary, though not necessarily hostile, prison or dwelling for the soul</note>.

Cheer yourself up, then, O my soul, against all the fears of your dissolution <note>original: "dissolution"; meaning the breaking apart of the body at death</note>. Your departure from this life is no more certain than the benefits you will gain from it; your existence shall not...

Oh, the cowardice of our unbelief, if we shrink at such a momentary purchase of eternity! How many people have we known who, for a false reputation of honor, have rushed into the jaws of Death, even when we are certain they could not return to enjoy that honor? And yet, do I tremble at a minute’s pain that shall grant me legal possession <term>feoffe</term> of that glory which I cannot help but enjoy forever? 

How ashamed I am to hear of the pagan *Socrates* encouraging himself against the fears of Death by his resolve to meet some famous people in that other world—and yet to feel myself shuddering <note>original: "shrugging"</note> at a short bout of pain that shall place me in the bliss-giving presence of the all-glorious **God**, and into the sight of the glorified humanity of my dear Redeemer

### SECTION III.

->*On the soul's perpetual wakefulness <note>original: "vigilancy"</note> and the enjoyment <note>original: "fruition"</note> of God.*<-

It is nothing other than a frantic dream of those misguided <note>original: "erroneous"</note> spirits who have imagined the "sleep of the soul"—a sleep so long and deep that it lasts from the evening of death <note>original: "dissolution"</note> until the morning of the resurrection. They claim that during all that time, the soul has no vision of God and no feeling of joy or pain. This is an error wickedly raked up from the ashes of those Arabic heretics whom *Origen* is said to have converted <note>original: "reclaimed"</note>. Since that time, it has been taken up (unless they have been slandered) by the Armenians and the *Fratricelli* <note>A radical Franciscan sect in the Middle Ages</note>; it was even once supported and encouraged by Pope *John* XXII (as Pope *Adrian* witnesses),

<summary>The author rejects the doctrine of "soul sleep," arguing that the soul remains conscious and active immediately after death rather than waiting in an unconscious state until the resurrection. He traces this "error" back to ancient Arabic heretics and certain medieval groups.</summary>

Away, then, with that impious frenzy concerning the soul’s mortality or its "sleep" in death. No, my soul, you only then begin to live; you do not truly awake until then. Whether, by virtue of this divine illumination, they know the specific events we encounter here below, it would be a bold person who would try to determine. Consider how many impoverished "great ones" here below possess what they call honor and riches, yet do not enjoy them—and if they have enjoyed them, they complain of boredom <note>original: "satiety"; the state of being fed or gratified to excess, often leading to a sense of disgust or weariness.</note> and worthlessness! 

Behold here a boundless range of perfect joy and constant blessedness, without any dilution and without interruption. Each person feels their own joy and feels the joy of others; everyone rejoices in God with a joy that is unspeakable and full of glory, most sweetly immersing themselves in a pure and complete bliss. This very sight of blessed souls is happiness, but oh, to experience the fulfillment <note>original: "fruition"; the state of bearing fruit or the pleasure that comes from use or possession.</note>! 

Go now, my soul, and after seeing this vision, dote upon those trivial profits and pleasures that previously enchanted you; and (if you can) stop yourself from longing for the possession of this blessed immortality. Go ahead and complain <note>original: "repine"</note> at the news of such an advantageous transition <note>original: "translation"; here referring to the soul being "translated" or moved from earth to heaven.</note>, and pity and lament the departure of those dear pieces of 
thy

<summary>The author contrasts the hollow, wearying riches of the earthly powerful with the boundless and shared joy of souls in heaven. He concludes by challenging his own soul to stop clinging to worldly pleasures and instead embrace the "advantageous transition" to eternal life.</summary>

### Section 6

*In which the glory of the Saints above consists, and how they are employed.*

Such is the place and such is the condition of the blessed. What is their work <note>original: "employment"</note>? How do they spend—not their time—but their eternity? How else but in the exercise of the perpetual act of their blessedness: seeing God, clinging to Him, and enjoying Him? <note>original: "vision, adhesion, fruition"; these are technical theological terms for the soul's direct experience of God.</note> Who does not know that there is a contract made between God and the reborn <note>original: "regenerate"</note> soul here below? Out of the commitment <note>original: "engagement"</note> of His mercy and love, He endows the soul with the precious graces of faith, hope, and charity <note>original: "charity" here refers to "caritas," or divine, selfless love.</note>. Faith is the means by which the soul knowingly understands <note>original: "apprehends"</note>

### Section 7

*In what relationship the departed Saints stand to us, and what regard they have for us.*

This is the blessedness <note>original: "felicity"</note> with which the separate souls of God’s chosen <note>original: "elect"</note> ones are endowed <note>original: "feoffed"—a legal term meaning to be granted possession of land or property</note> forever. But, in the meantime, what relationship do they have with their former partners—these human bodies? What is their relationship to these pitiful <note>original: "forlorn"</note> companions of their earthly journey <note>original: "pilgrimage"</note> and struggle <note>original: "warfare"</note>? Do they despise these "houses of clay" in which they once lived? Or have they, like Pharaoh’s cupbearer <note>original: "Pharaoh's Courtier"; a reference to Genesis 40:23, where the cupbearer forgot Joseph after being released from prison.</note>, forgotten their fellow prisoner?

Therefore, the soul was no more reluctant to leave this old partner during their dissolution <note>death</note> than it is now eager to meet it again, knowing well how much happier the condition will be when they meet compared to when they formerly parted. ### Section VIII

->*The reunion of the body and the soul, both glorified.*<-

See, then, such a happy reunion—one that this material world is not capable of experiencing (until the last fire <note>original: "last fire"; a reference to the theological belief that the physical world will be purified by fire at the end of time before the creation of the "new heaven."</note> has refined it)—of a blessed soul met with a glorified body to populate the new heaven. Who can help but rejoice in spirit to foresee such a glorious communion of perfected Saints?

...Saints? To see their bodies with a clear brightness, free from all earthly opacity; with agility, free from all dullness; with subtlety <term>subtlety: in this context, the quality of being refined or spiritualized rather than dense and heavy</term>, free from coarseness; with impassibility <term>impassibility: the theological state of being unable to suffer pain, injury, or decay</term>, beyond the reach of annoyance or corruption? 

There and then you shall, O my soul, looking through clarified eyes, see and rejoice to see that glorious body of your dear God and Savior, which he took on here below, and in which he worked out the great task of your redemption. There you shall see the radiant bodies of all those eminent Saints whose graces you were accustomed to wonder at and weakly wish to imitate. There I shall meet with the visible partners of that same unspeakable glory: my once dear parents, children, and friends. ## Of the Evil Angels.

Surely this may seem like a slight representation <note>original: "sleight representation"; meaning a small or weak illustration</note> of the condition of apostate angels and reprobate souls <note>original: "reprobate souls"; those rejected by God and destined for damnation</note>. Their region is the kingdom of darkness; they have only enough light to see that they are eternally miserable, and they are not capable of the slightest glimpse of comfort or relief <note>original: "mitigation"</note>. 

But just as it happens with those who carry their own light through a dark night—they are easily spotted by an enemy waiting for them, and a good aim may be taken at them even while that enemy lurks unseen—so it is with us in these spiritual ambushes by the infernal powers. Their darkness and our light give them a significant advantage over us. The same power that clears and strengthens the eyes of our souls to see the surpassing glories of the good angels can also enable us to pierce through that hellish obscurity and to discern <note>original: "descrie"; to catch sight of or discover</note>...

In their first creation, there were no **angels** except those of light. The idea that any of them should bring evil with them from the first moment of their existence <note>original: "beeing"</note> is the long-rejected heresy of *Manes* <note>Manes (or Mani, c. 216–274 AD) was the founder of Manichaeism, which taught a dualistic struggle between a good spiritual world of light and an evil material world of darkness</note>, a man whose name fits his character <note>The author likely alludes to the Greek word *mania* (madness), a common disparagement of Manes by early church fathers</note>. And if *Prateolus* <note>Gabriel Prateolus (1511–1588) was a French scholar who wrote a famous encyclopedia of heresies titled "Elenchus Haereticorum Omnium"</note> is to be believed, this was also the view of the **Trinitarians** <note>In this context, the author is likely citing Prateolus’s claim about a specific radical sect rather than mainstream Christian Trinitarianism</note>. Indeed, it is blasphemy—throwing mud in the face of the most pure and holy **Deity**. 

For what can come from absolute goodness except good? And if any of those spirits could have been evil from the start, where could they claim to have gotten it? Either there must be a dominant original principle of evil, or evil must be derived from the fountain of infinite goodness—both of which are monstrous acts of impiety. All were once glorious spirits; sin changed their character <note>original: "hue"</note> and made many

# Of the Evil Angels.

<margin>Book 3.</margin> ...many of them ugly devils. Immediately, I am inclined to think: Lord, how could sin enter the world? How could it enter into angels? God made all things good; sin could not be his work. How could the good that he created produce the evil which he hates? Even this curious question <note>original: "curiosity"</note> must be answered.

When the great God intended to create his noblest creatures, he saw fit to produce them in the closest likeness to himself. Therefore, he endowed <note>original: "indue"</note> them with perfection of understanding and freedom of will. If either of these had been missing, there could have been no excellence in those who were intended to be the best. This is how he created his angels. 

Because their will was made free, they had power over their own inclinations. In some of them, those free inclinations swayed them away from that highest goal <note>original: "end"</note> at which they should have solely aimed...

<margin>Section 1.</margin>
This was the beginning of sin. Just as it happens with "efficient causes" <note>original: "causes efficient"; in logic, the agent or force that brings something about</note>, when the secondary agent swerves from the order and direction of the primary <note>original: "principal"</note> one, a fault immediately follows. For example, when a leg, because of crookedness, fails to perform the motion which the "appetitive power" <note>the faculty of desire or will that moves the body</note> commanded <note>original: "injoined"</note>, a limp immediately results. It is the same with "final causes" <note>original: "finall causes"; the ultimate purpose or goal for which something exists</note>—as Thomas Aquinas acutely observed—when the secondary goal is not kept subordinate to the primary and highest goal, a sin of the will arises. The object of the will is always "good"; but if a supposed and self-interested <note>original: "self respective"</note> good is allowed to take precedence over <note>original: "take the wall of"</note> the best and absolute good, the will immediately becomes corrupt <note>original: "vicious"</note>. Therefore, no fault can possibly occur in the will of someone who proposes to themselves as their on-
ly

# Of the Evil Angels

<margin>Book 3.</margin>...as *Palladius* <note>Palladius of Galatia (c. 363–420), a bishop and historian of early Christian monasticism who wrote the *Lausiac History*</note> reports, he dared to appear and demand worship—and received it—under the form of the Lord of Life, who is blessed forever.

How pointless is the observation of those authors who claim the difference between the appearances of good angels and evil ones is this: that good angels choose the forms of either beautiful people or clean and harmless creatures. Woe is me! What a doleful, what a dreadful spectacle is this which is now presented to my soul? I would not know you properly if I did not believe, along with the holy Chrysostom <note>original: "Chrysostome"; referring to St. John Chrysostom (c. 347–407 AD), an influential Early Church Father</note>, that the utter loss of your presence alone is as painful as a thousand hells.

The very sin of the damned is no small part of their hell. Just as all their powers, parts, and faculties are like so many subjects of their unbearable <note>original: "insupportable"</note> pain and torture, so out of that insufferable extremity, they conceive a desperate indignation and hatred against God. They do not hate him as he is in himself—infinitely good (for goodness cannot be an object of hate)—but as he is to them: a severe, though most just, avenger of sin. To this is always added a will obstinately fixed in evil. While they were in their earthly life <note>original: "in their way"</note>, they had the possibility of being reclaimed <note>original: "reclamation"</note>. Now that they are at the end of their journey <note>original: "*in termino*"; a theological term referring to the final, unchangeable state of a soul after death</note>, they can be nothing other than what they are. ### Section XII.

**The Comparison of both worlds: and how our thoughts and affections should be occupied with the Invisible World.**

Now then, having taken a view of both worlds—of the material world through the eyes of sense and reason, and of the invisible world through the eyes of reason and faith—I cannot help but wonder at <note>original: "admire"</note> God in both, and both of them in God. Yet I wonder at the invisible world so much more, as it is infinitely beyond the other. For God himself is the reality <note>original: "world of this world," meaning the essence or animating force behind the physical world</note> of this world. While in the material world we wonder at him through his creatures <note>In this context, "creatures" refers to all created things, including animals, plants, and humans</note>, in this immaterial world we wonder at him in himself. Now, he himself must necessarily be infinitely more

As for the parts of the created, but **Invisible** world, it must be said that its light-filled <note>original: "lightsome"</note> part has more glory than any piece of the material world is capable of possessing. On the contrary, the dark and empty <note>original: "privative," meaning a region defined by the total absence or loss of light and goodness</note> region of the **Invisible** world has infinitely more horror than the other. For what is the worst and most dismal darkness of this visible world but a deprivation of the light of the sun? In contrast, the darkness of this spiritual world is a total deprivation of the sight of **God**, joined with an inconceivable anguish. consideration of their blessed condition? These saints have now attained the fulfillment of their hopes—glory and bliss without end. Lastly, how our joy should be seasoned with a cautious fear when we cast our eyes upon those objects of dread and horror: the principalities and powers of darkness <note>original: "principalities and powers of darkness," a biblical reference to high-ranking demons or fallen angels</note>. <header>whole Discourse.</header>
<sig>L 3</sig>

<margin>SECTION 2.</margin>

How much should our hearts be filled beforehand <note>original: "pre-possessed"</note> with a most earnest and watchful care to resist all the dangerous assaults of those wicked spirits, and to prevent the danger <note>original: "perill"</note> of our own similarly miserable <note>original: "like-wofull"</note> destruction? If we make this use of our existence <note>original: "beeing"</note> in this visible world, we are happy that we ever entered it, and even happier in our departure from it. For by having used this world as if we did not use it <note>A reference to 1 Corinthians 7:31, advising believers not to be engrossed by earthly things</note>, we shall enjoy the other world as those who will possess it forever—and in it, all glory, honor, and immortality.

Look then, O my soul, at the glorious world toward which you are now aspiring—indeed, the world which you are now entering. Fix yourself there, never to be moved. Look down upon these inferior things with a passing <note>original: "overly," here meaning a slight or superficial disdain from a higher perspective</note> contempt; forget what is past, as if it had never been. Say a willing farewell to this visible world; where, just as your Creator

<meta>catchword: Creator</meta>

<summary>The author exhorts the reader to resist demonic temptation and to use their time in the physical world as a preparation for the eternal one. The soul is encouraged to look past earthly matters and fix its gaze entirely on the glory of heaven.</summary>
<keywords>spiritual vigilance, demonic assault, immortality, worldliness, soul's journey, heaven, Creator</keywords>

BOOK 3.

Creator has a rightful <note>original: "just interest"</note> claim to glory, because its substance is the marvelous workmanship of his hands; yet Satan (called the Prince of this world) claims no small share because of its sinful depravity.

Farewell, then, to you frivolous and empty <note>original: "windy"</note> honors, which are always controlled by others rather than ourselves. You have always been no less fickle than the breath you depended upon. Your chief use has been for temptation—to puff up the heart with a proud, exaggerated opinion <note>original: "conceit"</note> of superiority over others—without ever rewarding that danger with any solid contentment.

Farewell to you, deceitful riches, which we cannot hold even when we have them, and which we cannot enjoy even while we hold them. And if we attempt <note>original: "offer and affect"</note> to enjoy them, is it not at the cost of our spiritual loss? For whatever love we choose to waste on you...

<summary>The author bids farewell to the vanities of the physical world, specifically empty honors and deceitful wealth, while acknowledging God as the world's creator and Satan as the source of its corruption.</summary>

<margin>SECTION 12.</margin>
...we withhold <note>original: "abate"</note> from him who is the true and all-sufficient good. Beyond what is needed for necessary use, we are never the better for you [riches], and often the worse. Your burden is more troublesome <note>original: "uneasy"</note> than your value is precious.

Farewell, pleasures (if I ever truly knew what you were), which have always tended <note>original: "been wont"</note> to provide more of a sting than honey. Your only purpose has openly been—under a pretense of delight <note>original: "delectation"</note>—to corrupt and weaken the mind, and to cause a distaste for all spiritual comforts. While the anticipation of you was somewhat delightful, the actual enjoyment <note>original: "fruition"</note> was unsatisfying, the loss <note>original: "loose"</note> of you was displeasing, and the memory of you is bothersome <note>original: "irksome"</note>.

...of Sir *James Pennyman*, available from all booksellers. <note>original: "James Fennant" (OCR error for Pennyman). Sir James Pennyman was a prominent Royalist during the English Civil War.</note>

**11.** *Bright Star* <note>original: *Clara Stella*</note>, by *Robert Heath*, Esquire. <note>Robert Heath's *Clara Stella* (1650) was a popular collection of poems.</note>

**12.** Doctor *Preston’s* **The Saints' Infirmities**. <note>original: "Doctor Prat". John Preston was a famous Puritan minister; this work focuses on the spiritual lapses of the godly.</note>

**13.** **A Commentary on the 109th Psalm**, written as an answer to the enemies of the Church, by *Thomas Wall*, Master of Arts and Vicar.

**14.** **A Catechism** containing the rules of the Christian Religion, by *Moses Wall*.

---

->**THE END.**<-
<note>original: *FINIS* (Latin for "the end")</note>
